<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0177Kz2F65VGoF5cDEoiNC6c`</summary>

```text
docs/paper/related_work.tex:5:TracePile \citep{chen2025tracepile} scaled trace-augmented training to 2.6M samples across multiple programming languages, demonstrating gains on code generation tasks.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:6:\citet{wang2025dcsh} conducted a comprehensive study of execution trace-based information for code LLMs across synthesis, repair, and reasoning tasks, finding that trace-based semantic information has limited and inconsistent utility---a finding our work extends by showing that for \emph{verdict prediction}, exception events are a clear dominant signal.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:7:NExT \citep{ni2024next} used self-training on execution trace chain-of-thought rationales, improving program repair on MBPP by 26.1\%.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:8:ET-CoT \citep{eguchi2025etcot} introduced sub-line granularity traces via PyTracify, achieving 70.0\% on CRUXEval-O with SFT on Qwen2.5-7B.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:9:\citet{jung2025execution} used debugger-extracted traces as grounded supervision for LLM reasoning, reducing overthinking while improving cross-domain transfer.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:10:\citet{ferrando2025execute} compared regular, compact, and dynamic scratchpad strategies, finding that compact traces (recording only changed variables) perform comparably to full traces---directly supporting our finding that complete value information is redundant.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:11:CRUXEval \citep{gu2024cruxeval} established benchmarks for code execution reasoning; CRUXEval-X \citep{xu2025cruxevalx} extended this to 19 programming languages.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:15:TRACED \citep{ding2024traced} showed that execution-aware pretraining improves vulnerability detection and clone detection, using encoder-based models with trace-augmented objectives.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:16:In automated patch correctness prediction, APPT \citep{appt2024} fine-tuned pre-trained models with BERT+LSTM achieving 79.7\% accuracy on Defects4J patches, while LLM4PatchCorrect \citep{zhou2024llm4patch} used Starcoder-7B zero-shot with execution traces reaching 84.4\%.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:17:More recently, \citet{yang2025apsg} proposed Graph-LoRA with attributed patch semantic graphs for parameter-efficient patch classification.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:18:FuseFL \citep{widyasari2024fusefl} fused SBFL rankings with test execution outcomes (including error messages) for fault localization, demonstrating that structured fault signals improve over raw inputs.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:19:\citet{foster2025mutation} deployed LLM-based equivalence detection at scale at Meta, achieving 0.95 precision on mutation testing.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:23:TraceCoder \citep{huang2026tracecoder} inserts diagnostic probes at selected locations rather than recording full traces, improving Pass@1 by 34.4\% for LLM-generated code debugging.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:24:InspectCoder \citep{wang2025inspectcoder} enables LLMs to actively control a debugger for strategic breakpoint placement and runtime state inspection, outperforming baselines by 5--60\%.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:25:ND-Slicer \citep{yadavally2024ndslicer} uses execution-aware pretraining to predict dynamic program slices without execution, achieving 81.3\% exact match.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:26:\citet{haque2025traces} found that raw execution traces provide limited benefit for program repair and that LLM-optimized prompt formats consistently outperform raw traces---supporting our finding that trace compression is essential.
docs/paper/related_work.tex:27:\citet{wu2026runtime} used selective variable snapshots in a multi-agent repair debate framework, further validating that targeted trace extraction outperforms full recording.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:149:Beyond accuracy, we evaluate whether trace conditions affect prediction calibration, motivated by recent findings that code LLM confidence is often poorly calibrated \citep{spiess2025calibration}.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:19:The VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}929, causing 91.7\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating trace content while preserving the verdict label---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \citep{yang2024generalization}.
docs/paper/method.tex:7:Unlike code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile} or output prediction \citep{gu2024cruxeval}, verdict prediction isolates the model's ability to detect faults from its ability to simulate execution, providing a cleaner lens on what trace information actually contributes.
docs/paper/method.tex:12:We sampled 10 candidate solutions per problem from Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct (temperature 0.8, top\_p 0.95) for 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} (164 problems) and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} (427 problems), executed each solution against canonical test cases with \texttt{sys.settrace} to obtain line-level execution traces and pass/fail verdicts, yielding 5,910 trace--verdict pairs (67\% pass, 33\% fail).
docs/paper/method.tex:67:\item \textbf{Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct} \citep{qwen2025coder}: Pretrained on 5.5T tokens. Serves as the primary model for all ablation experiments.
docs/paper/method.tex:68:\item \textbf{DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct} \citep{deepseek2024coder}: Pretrained on 2T tokens. Used for cross-model validation to test whether findings generalize beyond a single model family.
docs/paper/method.tex:71:The two models differ primarily in pretraining data volume (5.5T vs.\ 2T tokens), providing a natural axis for evaluating whether trace signal utility is model-dependent \citep{dong2024composition}.
docs/paper/method.tex:76:We apply LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} (rank=16, $\alpha$=32, dropout=0.05) to all linear layers and train for 3 epochs with batch size 4, gradient accumulation 8 (effective batch size 32), learning rate $2 \times 10^{-4}$ with linear warmup and linear decay, warmup ratio 0.1, and weight decay 0.01.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:37:All experiments use the HuggingFace TRL \texttt{SFTTrainer} with LoRA \citep{hu2022lora} applied to all linear layers.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:4:Recent work has explored trace-augmented training for code generation \citep{chen2025tracepile}, execution-aware pretraining \citep{ding2024traced}, trace-based code understanding \citep{wang2025dcsh}, and self-training on trace chain-of-thought \citep{ni2024next}.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:7:\citet{wang2025dcsh} found that no single trace representation consistently outperforms others on generation-oriented tasks, concluding that the utility of execution traces is task- and format-dependent.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:13:Using 591 problems from HumanEval \citep{chen2021evaluating} and MBPP-sanitized \citep{austin2021program} with model-generated solutions and execution traces, we decompose a multi-component trace representation (VOTA: exception events + assertion-point comparisons + loop compression) into individual components and evaluate each in isolation across two models: Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct and DeepSeek-Coder-6.7B-Instruct.
```

```text
docs/paper/experiments.tex:6:All experiments use our trace--verdict dataset (\S\ref{sec:dataset}) with $N{=}1{,}190$ test samples; accuracy is reported with Wilson 95\% confidence intervals unless stated otherwise.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:34:Table~\ref{tab:cross_model} reveals a striking asymmetry.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:43:Per-class analysis (Appendix~\ref{sec:per_class}) reveals the mechanism: DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, systematically defaulting to \texttt{pass}---in contrast to Qwen VOTA, which achieves 100\% fail recall.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:50:Because the \texttt{exception\_only} test set contains 48.99\% duplicate inputs (607 unique out of 1,190; \S\ref{sec:dataset}), the reported CIs assume $n{=}1{,}190$ independent trials.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:57:Table~\ref{tab:ablation} arranges conditions into a two-tier hierarchy: Tier~1 signals (\texttt{ae\_only}, \texttt{exception\_only}) that individually achieve ${\geq}$99.8\% accuracy, and Tier~2 signals (\texttt{loop\_only}) that provide moderate improvement over the no-trace baseline.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:108:As shown in Table~\ref{tab:density}, the decomposition attributes 26\% of the gap (+4.46pp, $p{<}0.0001$, bootstrap 95\% CI $[{+}2.35, {+}6.55]$) to \emph{gradient dilution}---the training signal is diluted across trace tokens that do not contribute to verdict prediction---and 74\% (+13.19pp) to \emph{information overload}---the model must process trace lines irrelevant to the fault at inference time.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:109:This two-factor decomposition is consistent across models: the VOTA cross-model gap (14.96pp, Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is confounded by training-time trace truncation on DeepSeek (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}), which amplifies information overload by removing diagnostic trace content from the majority of training samples.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:140:As shown in Table~\ref{tab:trace_length} and Figure~\ref{fig:trace_length}, all four trace length conditions fall within a 5pp range (77.2\%--81.8\%) with broadly overlapping confidence intervals.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:143:The result reinforces the information density interpretation (\S\ref{sec:density}): the gap between full traces and VOTA is driven by the \emph{content} of the trace (irrelevant lines diluting the diagnostic signal), not its \emph{length}.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:144:In contrast, VOTA's compressed representation concentrates the diagnostic signal into fewer tokens, making it sensitive to truncation when those tokens are removed (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
docs/paper/experiments.tex:150:Figure~\ref{fig:calibration} shows reliability diagrams for four conditions on Qwen-2.5-7B.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:170:Table~\ref{tab:calibration} shows that calibration quality mirrors the Tier~1/2 hierarchy.
docs/paper/experiments.tex:176:Figure~\ref{fig:cross_model_gap} visualizes the cross-model gap pattern, showing that exception-based conditions maintain tight cross-model agreement while VOTA and lower-tier conditions diverge.
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:9:In contrast, the full VOTA representation degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek---a gap confounded by training-time trace truncation (the majority of DeepSeek VOTA samples exceed the context window; \S\ref{sec:cross_model}) and model capability differences, which cannot be disentangled in the current design.
docs/paper/conclusion.tex:13:A trace length ablation confirms that input truncation is not the bottleneck: full-trace accuracy remains in a narrow 77--82\% band regardless of token budget (\S\ref{sec:trace_length}).
docs/paper/discussion.tex:12:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/discussion.tex:16:The cross-model gap pattern (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}
docs/paper/discussion.tex:22:The resulting 0.50pp cross-model gap (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:36:The contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\% to 84.45\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\S\ref{sec:cross_model})---the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.
docs/paper/discussion.tex:61:Fourth, the initial DeepSeek \texttt{full\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow (the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2{,}048 tokens, truncating the verdict label); a re-run with extended context yields 79.16\% (CI [76.80\%, 81.30\%]), 2.60pp below Qwen (Table~\ref{tab:cross_model}).
docs/paper/method.tex:17:Table~\ref{tab:dedup} reports the duplication rates across formats.
docs/paper/method.tex:41:We define seven input conditions that systematically vary the trace information available to the model (Figure~\ref{fig:trace_overview}):
docs/paper/appendix.tex:4:Table~\ref{tab:per_class} reports per-class precision, recall, and F1 for all experimental conditions.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:8:\caption{Per-class precision, recall, and F1 for all experimental conditions (seed 42). DeepSeek VOTA achieves 100\% fail precision but only 45.43\% fail recall, indicating systematic bias toward predicting \texttt{pass}. DeepSeek \texttt{exception\_only} seed 42 achieves 100\% fail recall, but seed 123 drops to 70.5\% (see Table~\ref{tab:full_deepseek}).}
docs/paper/appendix.tex:36:Table~\ref{tab:hyperparams} summarizes the training hyperparameters shared across all conditions.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:74:Table~\ref{tab:train_time} reports per-condition training wall-clock times.
docs/paper/appendix.tex:162:Tables~\ref{tab:full_qwen} and \ref{tab:full_deepseek} report the complete experimental results across all conditions, models, and seeds.
docs/paper/introduction.tex:24:[Omitted long matching line]
docs/paper/introduction.tex:28:\item \textbf{Exception signal transfer}: We show that exception events alone achieve 99.83\% $\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity; even the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\%) by 6.64pp (\S\ref{sec:cross_model}).
docs/paper/introduction.tex:29:\item \textbf{Information density decomposition}: We decompose the full-trace-to-VOTA gap into gradient dilution (26\%) and information overload (74\%), identifying the primary bottleneck for trace-based prediction (\S\ref{sec:density}).
docs/paper/introduction.tex:30:\item \textbf{Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy}: Component ablation reveals that Tier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\geq}$99.8\% accuracy while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach only 85.6\% (\S\ref{sec:ablation}).
```

  "old_string": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}",
  "new_string": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\n\nTo test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench}---a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).\nAfter filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\\\\nVOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\\\\nno\\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is preserved, and exception\\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:debugbench}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (100.00\\%) $\\geq$ VOTA (99.77\\%) $>$ \\texttt{no\\_trace} (96.71\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.\n\nThe absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\\%---compared to 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP---leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.\nThis ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.\nIn contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.\n\nThese results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach---the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is consistent across both datasets---but the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.\nThe 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\\_trace 75.13\\% $\\to$ exception\\_only 99.83\\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals."
}

    "oldString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}",
    "newString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_cross_model_gap}\n\\caption{Cross-model accuracy gap (Qwen $-$ DeepSeek) across trace conditions. Exception-based signals transfer across models (0.50pp gap), while VOTA is model-dependent (14.96pp gap).}\n\\label{fig:cross_model_gap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Dataset Generalization: DebugBench}\n\\label{sec:debugbench}\n\nTo test whether exception-based verdict prediction generalizes beyond LLM-generated solutions, we evaluate on DebugBench \\citep{tian2024debugbench}---a dataset of human-written programs with synthetically injected bugs (single-point mutations such as operator replacement, variable misuse, and missing statements).\nAfter filtering for executable Python samples with valid traces, we retain 426 samples with a near-balanced class distribution (majority baseline: 51.4\\%).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Condition} & \\textbf{Acc (\\%)} & \\textbf{95\\% CI} \\\\\n\\midrule\nexception\\_only & \\textbf{100.00} & [99.14, 100.00] \\\\\nVOTA & 99.77 & [99.30, 100.00] \\\\\nno\\_trace & 96.71 & [94.80, 98.40] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMajority baseline & 51.41 & --- \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Generalization to DebugBench ($N{=}426$, Qwen-2.5-7B, seed=42). Wilson 95\\% CIs. The component ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is preserved, and exception\\_only achieves perfect accuracy.}\n\\label{tab:debugbench}\n\\end{table}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:debugbench} shows that the component ranking from the main experiments is preserved: \\texttt{exception\\_only} (100.00\\%) $\\geq$ VOTA (99.77\\%) $>$ \\texttt{no\\_trace} (96.71\\%).\nThe \\texttt{exception\\_only} condition achieves perfect accuracy, confirming that the exception-based representation generalizes to a different fault distribution without degradation.\n\nThe absolute margins are much narrower than on the main dataset: the \\texttt{no\\_trace} baseline already achieves 96.71\\%---compared to 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP---leaving only 3.29pp of headroom for trace-based improvement.\nThis ceiling effect reflects a fundamental difference in fault characteristics: DebugBench bugs are synthetically injected single-point mutations, which produce code that is structurally dissimilar from the original correct solution; a model can often identify the fault from source code alone.\nIn contrast, the HumanEval+MBPP solutions are LLM-generated, producing natural errors (logic mistakes, edge-case failures) where the faulty and correct solutions may be structurally similar, making code-only verdict prediction substantially harder.\n\nThese results support exception-based verdict prediction as a generalizable approach---the method does not degrade on an out-of-distribution dataset, and the exception dominance ranking (exception\\_only $\\geq$ VOTA $>$ no\\_trace) is consistent across both datasets---but the narrow DebugBench margins are insufficient to independently validate trace importance.\nThe 24.70pp trace lift on the main dataset (no\\_trace 75.13\\% $\\to$ exception\\_only 99.83\\%) remains the primary evidence for the value of exception-based trace signals.",

  "old_string": "These results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to other datasets (DebugBench, LiveCodeBench), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.",
  "new_string": "A cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench (\\S\\ref{sec:debugbench}) confirms that the exception dominance ranking generalizes: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% accuracy ($N{=}426$), preserving the component ordering from the main experiments.\nThe narrow no-trace baseline margin on DebugBench (96.71\\% vs.\\ 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP) reflects the structural distinctiveness of synthetically injected bugs, reinforcing that the main dataset's 24.70pp trace lift is the primary evidence for trace signal value.\n\nThese results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to additional datasets (LiveCodeBench, real-world CI logs), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction."
}

    "oldString": "These results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to other datasets (DebugBench, LiveCodeBench), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.",
    "newString": "A cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench (\\S\\ref{sec:debugbench}) confirms that the exception dominance ranking generalizes: \\texttt{exception\\_only} achieves 100\\% accuracy ($N{=}426$), preserving the component ordering from the main experiments.\nThe narrow no-trace baseline margin on DebugBench (96.71\\% vs.\\ 75.13\\% on HumanEval+MBPP) reflects the structural distinctiveness of synthetically injected bugs, reinforcing that the main dataset's 24.70pp trace lift is the primary evidence for trace signal value.\n\nThese results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to additional datasets (LiveCodeBench, real-world CI logs), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe presented the first systematic study of what execution trace components drive verdict prediction performance.\nThrough component ablation across two code LLMs, we established three findings:\n\n\\textbf{(1) Exception signal transfer.}\nException events alone achieve 99.83\\% $\\pm$ 0.00 on Qwen (3 seeds) and 99.33\\% on 2/3 DeepSeek seeds, with one outlier at 91.09\\% from LoRA initialization sensitivity.\nEven the outlier exceeds VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp, confirming exception events as the most effective component across models; training stability is a secondary factor for smaller models.\nIn contrast, the full VOTA representation degrades by 14.96pp on DeepSeek---a gap confounded by training-time trace truncation (the majority of DeepSeek VOTA samples exceed the context window; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}) and model capability differences, which cannot be disentangled in the current design.\n\n\\textbf{(2) Information density decomposition.}\nThe 17.65pp gap between full traces and VOTA decomposes into gradient dilution (26\\%, from training on irrelevant trace tokens) and information overload (74\\%, from processing irrelevant trace lines at inference).\nA trace length ablation confirms that input truncation is not the bottleneck: full-trace accuracy remains in a narrow 77--82\\% band regardless of token budget (\\S\\ref{sec:trace_length}).\nThe primary bottleneck is trace content quality, not quantity or loss design.\n\n\\textbf{(3) Two-tier diagnostic hierarchy.}\nTier~1 signals (exception events, assertion points) individually achieve ${\\geq}$99.8\\% accuracy, while Tier~2 signals (loop compression) reach 85.6\\%.\nCombining tiers in VOTA slightly degrades performance (99.41\\%), confirming that additional trace components introduce noise rather than complementary signal.\n\nThese results yield a practical design principle: for verdict prediction, extract the highest-specificity diagnostic signal and discard everything else.\nFuture work should test whether exception dominance extends to other datasets (DebugBench, LiveCodeBench), model scales, and classification tasks beyond verdict prediction.",

    "newString": "First, our primary experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); while a cross-dataset evaluation on DebugBench (\\S\\ref{sec:debugbench}) confirms that exception dominance generalizes to synthetically injected bugs, generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or real-world fault distributions remains untested.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\n\\subsection{Why Exception Events Dominate}\n\nVerdict prediction is fundamentally a fault detection task: the model must determine whether execution deviated from expected behavior.\nException events are the most explicit signal of such deviation---structured diagnostic moments that encode the nature and location of a fault.\nUnlike loop compressions or assertion-point values, which require the model to integrate information across multiple trace elements, exception events provide a self-contained verdict cue.\nThis explains why \\texttt{exception\\_only} matches or exceeds VOTA: adding non-exception trace components dilutes the model's attention on the diagnostic signal rather than providing complementary information.\n\nWe acknowledge that exception-based verdict prediction is, at its core, fault indicator presence detection: the model learns to map the presence or absence of a structured error message to a binary label, which is close to trivially solvable for any instruction-tuned LLM.\nThe Tier~1 accuracy of ${\\geq}$99.8\\% is therefore unsurprising in isolation.\nThe contribution of this finding lies not in demonstrating that a strong signal yields high accuracy, but in two less obvious results: (a)~adding more trace information---loop compressions, assertion-point values---actively \\emph{hurts} performance (VOTA $<$ \\texttt{exception\\_only}, Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}), quantifying an information density dilution effect that has not been previously measured; and (b)~this dilution is confounded by tokenizer-driven trace truncation on DeepSeek: the DeepSeek tokenizer produces longer sequences than Qwen for identical inputs, causing the majority of VOTA training samples to exceed the context window (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model}); the verdict label is preserved, but the trace content is truncated, depriving the model of diagnostic information and contributing to the collapse of DeepSeek VOTA fail recall to 45.43\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:per_class}) while Qwen maintains 100\\%.\n\n\\subsection{Simplicity as Relative Robustness}\n\nThe cross-model gap pattern (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) suggests a design principle: \\emph{trace representations should match the model capability floor, not the ceiling.}\nException events are consistently the most effective component on both models, and their structural simplicity---a fixed-format error message---ensures that the relative advantage over richer representations holds across all seeds.\nTwo of three DeepSeek seeds match Qwen-level accuracy (99.33\\%); the outlier (seed 123, 91.09\\%) is attributable to LoRA initialization landing in a suboptimal basin (fail recall = 70.5\\%), not to a failure of the exception signal itself---even this outlier outperforms VOTA (84.45\\%) by 6.64pp.\nThe VOTA cross-model gap is confounded by two factors: (1)~tokenizer efficiency---the DeepSeek tokenizer averages 2{,}626 tokens per VOTA sample versus Qwen's 1{,}929, causing 91.7\\% of training samples to exceed the 2{,}048-token context window, truncating trace content while preserving the verdict label---and (2)~base model capability differences, consistent with findings that SFT generalization depends on pretrain quality \\citep{yang2024generalization}.\nThe two factors cannot be disentangled in the current experimental design.\nOur \\texttt{exception\\_only} design sidesteps the truncation confound entirely: inputs average ${\\sim}$200 tokens, well below the 2{,}048-token limit, so no training samples are truncated on either model.\nThe resulting 0.50pp cross-model gap (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}) is therefore a clean measure of model capability differences, making the ``capability floor'' principle directly testable.\nThe practical implication is that trace-based systems should default to the simplest sufficient signal; for smaller models, training stability (multi-seed selection, warmup schedules) is a secondary but addressable concern.\n\n\\subsection{Training Dynamics and Loss Decoupling}\n\nThe DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} results reveal two instructive training dynamics.\nFirst, epoch-level analysis shows mild degradation in 2/3 seeds: seed 42 peaks at 99.83\\% after epoch~1 but settles at 99.33\\% by epoch~3 ($-$0.50pp), seed 123 degrades from 91.43\\% to 91.09\\% ($-$0.34pp), while seed 456 shows no epoch degradation (99.33\\% at both epoch~1 and epoch~3).\nEarly stopping may yield marginal gains for some seeds, but the epoch effect is small (${\\leq}$0.5pp) compared to the seed effect (8.24pp between seeds 42 and 123).\n\nSecond, seeds 123 and 456 converge to nearly identical final training losses (0.122 and 0.123 respectively), yet their accuracies differ by 8.24pp (91.09\\% vs.\\ 99.33\\%).\nThe critical difference lies in fail recall: seeds 42 and 456 achieve 100\\%, while seed 123 achieves only 70.5\\%---the LoRA initialization in seed 123 fails to learn the exception$\\to$fail mapping for a substantial fraction of failing samples, even though the per-token loss appears converged.\nThis demonstrates that token-level training loss can mask fundamental differences in sample-level classification ability, suggesting a parallel to the eval-loss/accuracy decoupling observed in DeepSeek VOTA (where eval loss rose 65\\% from epoch 1 to 3 while accuracy improved by 6.3pp) at the training phase.\nThe practical recommendation is twofold: (1)~use task-specific metrics (accuracy, fail recall) for checkpoint and seed selection rather than training or evaluation loss, and (2)~for deployment on smaller models, train multiple seeds and select the best, as a single run may not reliably converge to the correct classification boundary.\n\nThe contrast with VOTA is instructive: DeepSeek VOTA accuracy monotonically increases from 78.15\\% to 84.45\\% across epochs, but is consistent with a performance limit associated with the high trace truncation rate (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_model})---the model progressively extracts signal from samples with intact trace content but cannot fully compensate for the information loss in the truncated majority.\nThe 84.45\\% accuracy reflects a combination of trace information loss and model capability constraints.\n\n\\subsection{Error Analysis}\n\\label{sec:error_analysis}\n\nSeed robustness testing revealed that the two errors made by \\texttt{exception\\_only} (accuracy = 99.83\\%, 2/1190 incorrect) are \\emph{identical across all three seeds} (42, 123, 456).\nBoth errors are false negatives on samples involving the \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} function (sample IDs 901 and 905): the ground truth is \\texttt{pass}, but the model predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis 100\\% error overlap ($\\sigma{=}0$) across seeds demonstrates that these are not stochastic training artifacts but \\emph{irreducible samples} at the boundary of what exception-based trace signals can resolve.\n\nManual inspection reveals the root cause: \\texttt{check\\_dict\\_case} (HumanEval/95) uses \\texttt{all()} over a generator expression, and Python's \\texttt{all()} short-circuits on the first \\texttt{True} value, raising \\texttt{GeneratorExit} to terminate the generator.\nBecause \\texttt{GeneratorExit} is a \\texttt{BaseException} subclass, the tracer records it as seven \\texttt{[ERROR] GeneratorExit} lines---even though the function executes correctly and the test passes.\nThe model observes these spurious error markers and reasonably but incorrectly predicts \\texttt{fail}.\nThis is not a model failure but a \\emph{representation ceiling}: the \\texttt{exception\\_only} format cannot distinguish genuine exceptions from benign \\texttt{BaseException} subclasses triggered by Python's iterator protocol.\nThe 99.83\\% accuracy is therefore a deterministic upper bound for this trace representation on this dataset, not an indication of insufficient model capacity.\n\n\\subsection{Limitations}\n\nOur study has several limitations.\nFirst, all experiments use a single dataset (591 problems from HumanEval and MBPP-sanitized); generalization to other languages, larger codebases, or different fault types remains untested.\nSecond, we evaluate only two models at the 7B parameter scale; the relationship between model size and trace signal utility is unexplored.\nThird, DeepSeek \\texttt{exception\\_only} exhibits seed-dependent variance: 2/3 seeds achieve 99.33\\% while seed 123 yields 91.09\\% ($\\sigma{=}4.76$pp, $n{=}3$), attributable to LoRA initialization sensitivity (fail recall = 70.5\\% for the outlier vs.\\ 100\\% for others).\nQwen shows perfect seed robustness ($\\sigma{=}0$, $n{=}3$).\nWhether training stabilization techniques (e.g., larger warmup ratio, LoRA rank adjustment) can eliminate DeepSeek's seed sensitivity is unexplored.\nOther conditions use a single seed.\nFourth, the initial DeepSeek \\texttt{full\\_trace} run was invalidated by context overflow (the DeepSeek tokenizer produced sequences exceeding 2{,}048 tokens, truncating the verdict label); a re-run with extended context yields 79.16\\% (CI [76.80\\%, 81.30\\%]), 2.60pp below Qwen (Table~\\ref{tab:cross_model}).\nFifth, the candidate solutions were generated by Qwen-2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct, which also serves as one of our evaluation models.\nThis does not affect verdict prediction---the task is to classify a \\emph{given} solution as pass or fail, not to generate solutions---but we note the overlap for transparency.\nMoreover, the pass/fail distribution in the dataset is determined by the generating model's coding ability; a stronger or weaker generator would yield a different class balance, potentially affecting task difficulty and the generalizability of our accuracy estimates.\nFinally, our analysis is limited to verdict prediction; whether exception dominance extends to other classification tasks (vulnerability detection, clone detection) or to generation tasks (program repair) is an open question.",
